\mathbf{P}_{\phi^{+}}:=\mathrm{Hom}^{0}(\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}

Then

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\phi^{+}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}^{0}(\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\psi^{+}}L_{\phi^{+}}\otimes\mathrm{Hom}^{0}(\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}},\mathrm{IC}_{\psi^{+}})^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\psi^{+}}L_{\phi^{+}}\otimes\mathrm{Hom}^{0}_{D_{Z^{+}}(\mathfrak{g}^{a})}(\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}},\mathrm{IC}_{\psi^{+}})

(4.42)

\displaystyle=L_{\phi^{+}}

Using (
4.39
),
\mathbf{P}_{\phi^{+}}=L_{\phi^{+}}
is a complete collection of mutually non-isomorphic simple
\mathrm{Hom}^{0}(\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}
-modules. These modules can be regarded as
\mathrm{Hom}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
-module via the algebra homomorphism (
4.40
) above, and they provide a complete collection of mutually non-isomorphic simple
\mathrm{Hom}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
-modules.

Therefore, we need to determine what kind of
\mathrm{IC}
-sheaves do occur in decomposition of
\widetilde{K}^{+}
.

We have a
Z^{+}
-equivariant isomorphism
Z^{+}\times_{Z}\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{+,a}
given by the action map:
\dot{m}:(z,x,(g,1)B)\mapsto(\mathrm{Ad}(z)x,z\cdot(g,1)B)
. Let
m:Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathfrak{g}^{a}\to\mathfrak{g}^{a}
be the action map:
(z,x)\mapsto\mathrm{Ad}(z)x
. Hence we have the
Z^{+}
-equivariant commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{+}\times_{Z}\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{+,a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathfrak{g}^{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}^{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

where
p_{1}
is the projection.
Then we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{+}

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}^{+}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}=\tilde{f}^{+}_{!}\dot{m}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{f}^{+}\circ\dot{m})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}=(m\circ p_{1})_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}

(4.43)

\displaystyle=m_{!}{p_{1}}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}

From the natural inclusion
i:\mathfrak{g}^{a}\to Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathfrak{g}^{a}
and
\dot{i}:\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{a}\to Z^{+}\times_{Z}\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{a}
, we have the induction equivalences:

i^{*}:D_{Z^{+}}(Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathfrak{g}^{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}D_{Z}(\mathfrak{g}^{a})

\dot{i}^{*}:D_{Z^{+}}(Z^{+}\times_{Z}\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}D_{Z}(\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{a})

Using the commutative diagram

(4.44)

\textstyle{Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathfrak{g}^{a}}
\textstyle{Z^{+}\times_{Z}\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}^{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\dot{i}}

and the fact that the induction equivalence commutes with
\tilde{f}_{!}
, we have

i^{*}{p_{1}}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}=\tilde{f}_{!}\dot{i}^{*}\underline{\mathbb{C}}=\tilde{f}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}

which means

(4.45)

{p_{1}}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}=(i^{*})^{-1}\tilde{f}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}

Denote

\widetilde{K}:=\tilde{f}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}

Combining (
4.43
) and (
4.45
), we have

(4.46)

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{+}

\displaystyle=m_{!}{p_{1}}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}=m_{!}(i^{*})^{-1}\tilde{f}_{!}\underline{\mathbb{C}}

(4.47)

\displaystyle=m_{!}(i^{*})^{-1}\widetilde{K}=\mathrm{Ind}_{Z}^{Z^{+}}\widetilde{K}

where
\mathrm{Ind}_{Z}^{Z^{+}}=\mathrm{Ind}_{Z*}^{Z^{+}}=\mathrm{Ind}_{Z!}^{Z^{+}}
because of
m_{!}=m_{*}
and
m^{*}=m^{!}
.

Denote the stabilizer of
y\in\mathfrak{g}^{a}
in
Z=Z_{G\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(a)
by
Z_{y}=\{(g,r)\in G\times\mathbb{C}^{*}|\mathrm{Ad}(g)\bar{t}=\bar{t},\mathrm{Ad}(g)y=r^{2}y\}
, where
a=(\bar{t},r_{0})
. Now we assume that
Z_{y}
is connected for all
y
.

Applying the equivariant decomposition theorem to the
Z
-equivariant proper map
\tilde{f}:\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{a}\to\mathfrak{g}^{a}
, we get

\widetilde{K}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z},\phi=(\mathbb{O},1)}L_{\phi}(i)\otimes\mathrm{IC}_{\phi}[i]

where
\phi=(\mathbb{O},1)
runs over
Z
-orbits
\mathbb{O}
.

Using the non-vanishing result of
\widetilde{K}
, we know that all
Z
-orbit
\mathrm{IC}_{\phi}
does occur in the decomposition of
\widetilde{K}
. Hence we have

(4.48)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ind}_{Z}^{Z^{+}}\widetilde{K}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z},\phi=(\mathbb{O},1)}L_{\phi}(i)\otimes\mathrm{Ind}_{Z}^{Z^{+}}\mathrm{IC}_{\phi}[i]

Lemma 4.13
.

Take a
Z
-orbit
\mathbb{O}
and
y\in\mathbb{O}
.

If
Z^{+}\cdot\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}
, we have
Z^{+}_{y}/Z_{y}\simeq\mathfrak{R}
and
\pi_{0}(Z^{+}_{y})\simeq\mathfrak{R}
, hence
\mathrm{Ind}_{Z}^{Z^{+}}\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O},1)}=\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O},1)^{+}}\oplus\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O},-1)^{+}}
, where
\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O},1)^{+}}
and
\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O},-1)^{+}}
denote the equivariant intersection cohomology sheaf on the
Z^{+}
-orbit
\mathbb{O}
corresponding to the trivial and sign representations of
\pi_{0}(Z^{+}_{y})\simeq\mathfrak{R}
, respectively.

If
Z^{+}\cdot\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
, we have
Z^{+}_{y}=Z_{y}
and
\pi_{0}(Z^{+}_{y})=\pi_{0}(Z_{y})=1
, hence
\mathrm{Ind}_{Z}^{Z^{+}}\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O},1)}=\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime},1)^{+}}
, where
\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime},1)^{+}}
denotes the equivariant intersection cohomology sheaf on the
Z^{+}
-orbit
\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
corresponding to the trivial representation of
\pi_{0}(Z^{+}_{y})
.

Proof.

The induction equivalence

i^{*}:D_{Z^{+}}(Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathfrak{g}^{a})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}D_{Z}(\mathfrak{g}^{a})

sends
\mathrm{IC}(Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathbb{O},1)
to
\mathrm{IC}(\mathbb{O},1)
.

Recall the action map
m:Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathfrak{g}^{a}\to\mathfrak{g}^{a}
.

If
Z^{+}\cdot\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}
, then
\mathrm{Supp}(m_{!}(\mathrm{IC}(Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathbb{O},1)))\subset\bar{\mathbb{O}}
and
m_{!}(\mathrm{IC}(Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathbb{O},1))|_{\mathbb{O}}=\mathcal{L}_{1}[\mathrm{dim}\mathbb{O}]\oplus\mathcal{L}_{-1}[\mathrm{dim}\mathbb{O}]
, where
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{-1}
are the local systems on
\mathbb{O}
corresponding to the trivial and sign representation of
\pi_{0}(Z^{+}_{y})\simeq\mathfrak{R}
. Since the action map
m
is proper and small, we have
m_{!}(\mathrm{IC}(Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathbb{O},1))=\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O},1)^{+}}\oplus\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O},-1)^{+}}
.

If
Z^{+}\cdot\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
, then
\mathrm{dim}\mathbb{O}=\mathrm{dim}\mathbb{O}^{\prime}=\mathrm{dim}\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{Supp}(m_{!}(\mathrm{IC}(Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathbb{O},1)))\subset\bar{\mathbb{O}}\sqcup\bar{\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}
and
m_{!}(\mathrm{IC}(Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathbb{O},1))|_{\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{L}_{1}[\mathrm{dim}\mathbb{O}]
where
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is the constant local system on
\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
corresponding to the trivial representation of
\pi_{0}(Z^{+}_{y})=1
. Since the action map
m
is proper and small, we have
m_{!}(\mathrm{IC}(Z^{+}\times_{Z}\mathbb{O},1))=\mathrm{IC}_{(\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime},1)^{+}}

Using
\mathrm{Ind}_{Z}^{Z^{+}}\mathrm{IC}(\mathbb{O},1)=m_{!}(i^{*})^{-1}\mathrm{IC}(\mathbb{O},1)
, we get the lemma. ∎

Thus we get the lemma:

Lemma 4.14
.

The semisimple complex
\widetilde{K}^{+}
consists of all simple
Z^{+}
-equivariant perverse sheaves on
\mathfrak{g}^{a}

and the theorem

Theorem 4.15
.

There is a bijection between simple
Z^{+}
-equivariant perverse sheaves on
\mathfrak{g}^{a}
and simple
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
-modules, given by

(4.49)

\displaystyle\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}}\mapsto\mathbf{P}_{\phi^{+}}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Hom}^{0}(\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}

\displaystyle=L_{\phi^{+}}

where
\phi^{+}=(\mathbb{O},\chi)
runs over all pairs consisting of a
Z^{+}
-orbit
\mathbb{O}
in
\mathfrak{g}^{a}
and an irreducible
Z^{+}
-equivariant local system
\chi
on
\mathbb{O}
.

Remark 4.16
.

One can prove that
\mathrm{Res}^{Z^{+}}_{Z}\mathrm{Ind}_{Z}^{Z^{+}}\widetilde{K}=\widetilde{K}\oplus\tau^{*}\widetilde{K}
and that there is an embedding of subalgebras
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K},\widetilde{K})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
given by
f\mapsto f+\tau^{*}f
. There is an isomorphism
\mathrm{Hom}^{0}(\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}},\widetilde{K})\to\mathrm{Hom}^{0}(\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}
given by
h\mapsto h+\tau^{*}h
, which is an isomorphism of
\operatorname{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K},\widetilde{K})
-modules.

Hence if
Z^{+}\cdot\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}
, then
\mathbf{P}_{(\mathbb{O},1)^{+}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{(\mathbb{O},-1)^{+}}
have the
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K},\widetilde{K})
-module structure as
\mathbf{P}_{(\mathbb{O},1)}
; if
Z^{+}\cdot\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{P}_{(\mathbb{O}\sqcup\mathbb{O}^{\prime},1)^{+}}
has the
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K},\widetilde{K})
-module structure as
\mathbf{P}_{(\mathbb{O},1)}\oplus\mathbf{P}_{(\mathbb{O}^{\prime},1)}
.

4.2.2.
Standard module

We define
E(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+}):=\oplus_{k}H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})
. Then
E(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})
can be viewed as a
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
-module via:

(4.50)

\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}\to\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})\to\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+},i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})

Let
Z^{+}_{y}
be the stabilizer of
y
in
Z^{+}
. Then
Z^{+}_{y}
acts naturally on the stalks
H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})
of each
Z^{+}
-equivariant IC sheaf in
\widetilde{K}^{+}
, and this action factors through
Z^{+}_{y}/(Z^{+}_{y})^{0}
. This induces an action of
Z^{+}_{y}/(Z^{+}_{y})^{0}
on
E(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})
which commutes with the
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
-module structure. Let
\rho
be an irreducible representation of
Z^{+}_{y}/(Z^{+}_{y})^{0}
, and denote by
E(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\rho}
the
\rho
-isotypical component. Hence
E(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\rho}
depends only on
\psi^{+}=(\mathbb{O},\rho)
, a
Z^{+}
-orbit
y\in\mathbb{O}
and an irreducible
Z^{+}
-equivariant local system
\rho
on
\mathbb{O}
. We call
\mathbf{E}_{\psi^{+}}:=E(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\rho}
a standard module.

Similarly the group
Z^{+}_{y}/(Z^{+}_{y})^{0}
acts naturally on the stalks
H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}})
, and we also have
H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}})^{\rho}
.

Now we consider the Grothendieck group of
\mathrm{Hom}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
-modules of finite dimension over
\mathbb{C}
. There is a natural filtration on
E(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})
by the cohomological degree
m
. The action
Z^{+}_{y}/(Z^{+}_{y})^{0}
commutes with
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}
and preserves the filtration.

Proposition 4.17
.

In the Grothendieck group of
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
-modules of finite dimension over
\mathbb{C}
, we have the equality
\mathbf{E}_{\psi^{+}}\simeq\bigoplus_{\phi^{+}}(\bigoplus_{k}H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}}))^{\rho}\otimes\mathbf{P}_{\phi^{+}}
, hence
m(\mathbf{P}_{\phi^{+}},\mathbf{E}_{\psi^{+}})=\sum_{k}\mathrm{dim}H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}})^{\rho}
.

Proof.

We have
(\mathrm{gr}H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}}))^{\rho}=\bigoplus_{\phi^{+}}(\bigoplus_{k}H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}}))^{\rho}\otimes L_{\phi^{+}}
, and the
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
-action on
(\mathrm{gr}H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}}))^{\rho}
factors through the projection

\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}\simeq\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}\to\mathrm{Hom}^{0}(\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}=\bigoplus_{\phi^{+}}\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}L_{\phi^{+}}

Hence
\mathbf{P}_{\phi^{+}}=L_{\phi^{+}}
occurs as a
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
-module in
(\mathrm{gr}H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}}))^{\rho}
exactly
\sum_{k}\mathrm{dim}H^{k}(i_{y}^{!}\mathrm{IC}_{\phi^{+}})^{\rho}
times.
∎

Take
\phi^{+}=(\mathbb{O},\rho)
, a
Z^{+}
-orbit
y\in\mathbb{O}
and an irreducible
Z^{+}
-equivariant local system
\rho
on
\mathbb{O}
. We have a morphism

(4.51)

\mathcal{H}^{*}(i^{!}_{\mathbb{O}}\widetilde{K}^{+})\to\mathcal{H}^{*}(i^{*}_{\mathbb{O}}\widetilde{K}^{+})

obtained by applying the functor
i^{*}
to the canonical morphism
i_{!}i^{!}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}
and using that
i^{*}i_{!}i^{!}\mathcal{F}=i^{!}\mathcal{F}
.

This morphism (
4.51
) is compatible with the
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})
action. Denote

L_{y,\rho}=\mathrm{Im}(\mathcal{H}^{*}_{y}(i^{!}_{\mathbb{O}}\widetilde{K}^{+})_{\rho}\to\mathcal{H}^{*}_{y}(i^{*}_{\mathbb{O}}\widetilde{K}^{+})_{\rho})

Proposition 4.18
.

The isomorphism
L_{\phi^{+}}\simeq L_{y,\rho}
intertwines the
\mathrm{Hom}(\widetilde{K}^{+},\widetilde{K}^{+})^{\tau}
-action.

Proof.

Denote
\psi^{+}=(\mathbb{O}_{\psi^{+}},\rho_{\psi^{+}})
,
\phi^{+}=(\mathbb{O}_{\phi^{+}},\rho_{\phi^{+}})
. If
\mathbb{O}_{\psi^{+}}\neq\mathbb{O}_{\phi^{+}}
, the map
i^{!}_{{\mathbb{O}}_{\psi^{+}}}\widetilde{K}^{+}\to i^{*}_{{\mathbb{O}}_{\phi^{+}}}\widetilde{K}^{+}
vanishes. Using the decomposition of
\widetilde{K}^{+}
, we have
L_{\phi^{+}}\simeq L_{y,\rho}
as vector spaces. Since
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}
preserves the filtration of
\mathcal{H}^{*}_{y}(i^{!}_{\mathbb{O}}\widetilde{K}^{+})
and a similar result holds in the
i^{*}_{\mathbb{O}}
-case, the action
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}
on
L_{y,\rho}
factors through
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}(\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}\to\mathrm{Hom}^{0}(\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}},\widetilde{K}^{+^{\prime}})^{\tau}=\bigoplus_{\phi^{+}}\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}L_{\phi^{+}}
.
∎

Hence we can view all simple modules
\mathbf{P}_{\phi^{+}}\simeq L_{\phi^{+}}\simeq L_{y,\rho}
as a simple quotient of the standard module
\mathbf{E}_{\phi^{+}}
.

4.2.3.
Induction theorem

Recall
\tilde{f}^{+}:\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{+,a}\to\mathfrak{g}^{a}
, and denote by
\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a}
the inverse image
(\tilde{f}^{+})^{-1}(y)
of
y
. Consider the Cartesian diagram:

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{i}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}^{+}_{y}}
\textstyle{\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{+,a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}^{+}}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{i}_{y}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}^{a}}

Then
H^{*}(\tilde{i}_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})=H^{*}(\tilde{i}_{y}^{!}\tilde{f}^{+}_{*}\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})=H^{*}((\tilde{f}^{+}_{y})_{*}\tilde{i}^{!}\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})=H^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a},\tilde{i}^{!}\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})=H^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a},D\tilde{i}^{*}D\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})=H^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a},D\tilde{i}^{*}(\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})^{*})=H^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a},D(\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})^{*})=H_{*}^{\{1\}}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a},\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})
.

We also have
f^{+}:\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\to\mathfrak{g}
and
f:\dot{\mathfrak{g}}\to\mathfrak{g}
. Denote by
\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+}
the inverse image
({f}^{+})^{-1}(y)
of y, and by
\mathcal{P}_{y}
the inverse image
{f}^{-1}(y)
of y. Then in our case
\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a}=\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+}
. Since
H_{c}^{\mathrm{odd}}(\mathcal{P}_{y},\dot{\mathcal{L}})=0
, we have
H_{c}^{\mathrm{odd}}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+},\dot{\mathcal{L}}^{+})=0
. Then

\displaystyle H_{*}^{\{1\}}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a},\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{C}_{a}\otimes_{H^{*}_{T_{a}}}H_{*}^{T_{a}}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a},\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{C}_{a}\otimes_{H^{*}_{M_{y}(a)^{0}}}H_{*}^{M_{y}(a)^{0}}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+,a},\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{C}_{a}\otimes_{H^{*}_{M_{y}(a)^{0}}}H_{*}^{M_{y}(a)^{0}}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+},\tilde{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})

(4.52)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbb{C}_{a}\otimes_{H^{*}_{M_{y}^{0}}}H^{M_{y}^{0}}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+},{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})

where
M_{y}
is the stabilizer of
y
in
G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
a=(\bar{t},r_{0})
, and
M_{y}(a)
is the stabilizer of
a
in
M_{y}
.

In
[
1
]
, we also have a
\mathbb{H}(G^{+})\times(Z_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(a,y)/Z_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}^{0}(a,y))
-module structure on
\mathbb{C}_{a}\otimes_{H_{M_{y}^{0}}}H^{M_{y}^{0}}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+},{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})
.

Theorem 4.19
.

There is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{H}(G^{+})\times(Z_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(a,y)/Z_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}^{0}(a,y))
-representation between
H^{*}(i_{y}^{!}\widetilde{K}^{+})
and
\mathbb{C}_{a}\otimes_{H_{M_{y}^{0}}}H^{M_{y}^{0}}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+},{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})
.

Proof.

For any reductive group
A
,
A
-variety
N
and
\mathcal{F}\in D_{A}(N)
, we have
H^{*}_{A}(N,\mathcal{F})\simeq\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A}(\mathbb{C}_{N},\mathcal{F})
, so
H^{*}_{A}(N,\mathcal{F})
has a
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
-module structure given by

(4.53)

\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A}(\mathbb{C}_{N},\mathcal{F})\times\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\to\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A}(\mathbb{C}_{N},\mathcal{F})

Let
A^{\prime}
be a reductive group and
\epsilon:A^{\prime}\to A
a group homomorphism. For an
A^{\prime}
-variety
N^{\prime}
and an
\epsilon
-map
s:N^{\prime}\to N
, we can define the functor
s^{*}:D_{A}^{\prime}(N^{\prime})\to D_{A}(N)
. We have the commutative diagram

(4.54)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A}(s_{!}\mathbb{C}_{N},\mathcal{F})\times\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A}(s_{!}\mathbb{C}_{N},\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C}_{N^{\prime}},s^{!}\mathcal{F})\times\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A^{\prime}}(s^{!}\mathcal{F},s^{!}\mathcal{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{A^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C}_{N^{\prime}},s^{!}\mathcal{F})}

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}=(G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*})/M_{y}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathfrak{g}
be defined by
(g_{1},\lambda)\mapsto\lambda^{-2}\mathrm{Ad}(g_{1})y
and
\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}}=(G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+})/M_{y}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\dot{h}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}
be defined by
(g_{1},\lambda,gB)\mapsto(\lambda^{-2}\mathrm{Ad}(g_{1})y,g_{1}gB)
.

We have the Cartesian diagram:

(4.55)

\textstyle{\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{h}}
\scriptstyle{{f}^{+}_{\mathcal{O}}}
\textstyle{\dot{\mathfrak{g}}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{f}^{+}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}}

Then

\displaystyle H^{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}_{*}(\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},\dot{h}^{*}{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})

\displaystyle=H_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}^{*}(\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},D\dot{h}^{*}({\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})^{*})=H_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}^{*}(\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},\dot{h}^{!}{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})

\displaystyle=H_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},({f}^{+}_{\mathcal{O}})_{*}\dot{h}^{!}{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})=H_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},h^{!}(f^{+})_{*}{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})=H_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},h^{!}K^{+})

Hence we have a
\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(K^{+},K^{+})
-action on
H^{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}_{*}(\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},\dot{h}^{*}{\dot{\mathcal{L}}}^{+})\simeq H_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}^{*}({\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}},h^{!}K^{+})
given by

(4.56)

\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{D_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\mathfrak{g})}(K^{+},K^{+})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h^{!}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Hom}^{*}_{D_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}})}(h^{!}K^{+},h^{!}K^{+})

Since
\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+}
is a closed
M_{y}^{0}
-stable subvariety of the
G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
-variety
\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}}
and the map
M_{y}^{0}\backslash G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+}\simeq\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}}
is an isomorphism of
G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
-variety, we have an induction equivalence
j_{1}^{*}:D_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}})\to D_{M_{y}^{0}}(\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+})
, where
\dot{j_{1}}:\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+}\hookrightarrow\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}}
.

We have the Cartesian diagram:

(4.57)

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{y}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{j_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{{f}^{+}_{y}}

Remark 4.20
.

Recall
a=(\bar{t},r_{0})
and
Z^{+}=Z_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(a)=Z_{G^{+}}(\bar{t})\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
. For a nilpotent element
y
, it is possible to choose an algebraic homomorphism
\varphi_{y}:\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\to G
such that
d_{\varphi_{y}}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}=y
. There is a homomorphism
Z_{G^{+}}(y)\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\to Z_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(y)
given by
(g,r)\mapsto g\varphi_{y}\begin{pmatrix}r&0\\
0&r^{-1}\end{pmatrix}
. Hence
\pi_{0}(Z^{+}_{y})=\pi_{0}(Z_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(a,y))=Z_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(a,y)/Z_{G^{+}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}^{0}(a,y)\simeq Z_{G^{+}}(\bar{t},y)/Z_{G^{+}}^{0}(\bar{t},y)=\pi_{0}(Z_{G^{+}}(\bar{t},y))
.

5.
Langlands parameters

5.1.
Connected case

In
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
, we can write a Levi subgroup as
\mathbb{M}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{k}}
. For
\pi_{i}\in\mathrm{Rep}(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
, we write
\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{k}\in\mathrm{Rep}(\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
for the normalized parabolic induction
i_{\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})}(\pi_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi_{r})
via a parabolic subgroup
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
containing
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
{\mathbb{B}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Let
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,
\varrho_{0}^{i}=|\cdot|_{F}^{z_{i}}
,
z_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\varrho_{1}^{i}=\varrho_{0}|\cdot|_{F}^{\frac{1-n_{i}}{2}}
,
\varrho_{2}^{i}=\varrho_{0}|\cdot|_{F}^{\frac{3-n_{i}}{2}}
,
\cdots
,
\varrho_{n_{i}}^{i}=\varrho_{0}|\cdot|_{F}^{\frac{n_{i}-1}{2}}
, which are one dimensional representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{1}(F)=F^{\times}
. Let
\Delta_{i}=[\varrho_{1}^{i},\varrho_{n_{i}}^{i}]
be a segment.
Then we have the unique irreducible quotient
\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{i}\rangle
of
\varrho_{1}\times\varrho_{2}\times\cdots\times\varrho_{n_{i}}
in
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i}}(F))
.

A sequence of segments
(\Delta_{1},\cdots,\Delta_{k})
is said to be ordered if for every
1\leq i<j\leq k
,
\Delta_{i}
does not precede
\Delta_{j}
.

Let
\pi\in\mathrm{Rep}(\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
. Recall that the socle (resp., cosocle) of
\pi
, denoted by
\mathrm{soc}(\pi)
(resp.,
\mathrm{cos}(\pi)
), is the largest semisimple subrepresentation (resp., quotient) of
\pi
.

For any ordered form
(\Delta_{1},\cdots,\Delta_{k})
of a multisegment
\mathfrak{m}
, the representation
\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{k}\rangle
has a unique irreducible quotient

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{cos}(\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{k}\rangle)

Then we get the Langlands classification of all irreducible representations in
Rep(\mathcal{G})^{[\mathcal{T},1_{triv}]}
.

Denote
\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
G=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, the dual group of
\mathcal{G}
.
Using

(5.2)

\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}:\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})^{[\mathcal{T},1_{triv}]}\simeq\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})^{(\mathcal{J},1_{triv})}\simeq\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{J}\backslash\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{J})-\mathrm{Mod}

(5.3)

\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{J}\backslash\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{J})\simeq\mathcal{H}(G,p^{1/2})

(5.4)

\Theta:\mathbb{H}(Z_{G}(t_{c}))-\mathrm{Mod}_{(\mathrm{log}t_{h},\mathrm{log}p^{1/2})}\simeq\mathcal{H}(G,v)-\mathrm{Mod}_{(t,p^{1/2})}

where
t=t_{c}t_{h}\in G
is semisimple, we can classify all irreducible representations in
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})^{(\mathcal{J},1_{triv})}
.

We have the Iwahori-Matsumoto involution
\mathrm{IM}
on
\mathbb{H}(Z_{G}(t_{c}))
given by

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{IM}(N_{w})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(w)N_{w}\quad

\displaystyle(w\in W)

\displaystyle\mathrm{IM}(\xi)

\displaystyle=-\xi\quad

\displaystyle(\xi\in\mathfrak{t}^{*})

For a representation
(\pi,V)
of
\mathbb{H}(Z_{G}(t_{c}))
, denote by
(\mathrm{IM}^{*}\pi,V)
the representation twisted by
\mathrm{IM}
:

(5.6)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{IM}^{*}\pi)(g)\cdot v:=\pi(\mathrm{IM}(g))\cdot v

Moreover, using the compatibility between these equivalences and induction, we can obtain a correspondence between the standard representations in
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})^{(\mathcal{J},1_{triv})}
and the standard modules in
\mathbb{H}(Z_{G}(t_{c}))-\mathrm{Mod}
.

Denote by
\nu_{n_{i}}
the irreducible
n_{i}
-dimensional representation of
\mathrm{SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
and set
\nu=\oplus_{i=1}^{k}\nu_{n_{i}}
. Let
y=d_{\nu}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
,
t^{\prime}=\times_{{n_{1}}}(p^{z_{1}})\times\cdots\times\times_{{n_{k}}}(p^{z_{k}})
,
t^{\prime}_{h}=|t^{\prime}|
,
\bar{t}=-\mathrm{log}t^{\prime}_{h}+d_{\nu}\begin{pmatrix}r_{0}&0\\
0&-r_{0}\end{pmatrix}
, where
r_{0}=\mathrm{log}p^{1/2}
.

Hence the standard module

\mathrm{IM}^{*}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1}
of
\mathbb{H}(Z_{G}(t_{c}))
corresponds to
\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{k}\rangle
in
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})^{[\mathcal{T},1_{triv}]}
. The irreducible quotient module
\mathrm{IM}^{*}M_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1}
corresponds to
\mathrm{cos}(\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{k}\rangle)
through (
5.2
) and (
5.4
).

Denote by
{}^{L}\mathcal{G}=G\times W_{F}
the
L
-group of
G
, where
W_{F}
is the Weil group of
F
.

Definition 5.1
.

A Langlands parameter of
\mathcal{G}
is a continuous group homomorphism

\phi:W_{F}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\to{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}}

such that

(1) it respects the projections to
W_{F}
for both
W_{F}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}}
;

(2) its restriction to
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is algebraic;

(3) the image of
W_{F}
consists of semisimple elements in
{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}}
.

Let
P({{}^{L}\mathcal{G}})
be the set of Langlands parameters of
\mathcal{G}
. We say two Langlands parameters are equivalent if they are conjugate under
G
, and we denote the set of equivalence classes of Langlands parameters of
\mathcal{G}
by
\Phi(\mathcal{G})
.

An infinitesimal parameter of
\mathcal{G}
is a continuous homomorphism

\lambda:W_{F}\to{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}}

such that it satisfies the condition (1) and (3) of the definition of Langlands parameters.

If
\phi\in P({{}^{L}\mathcal{G}})
, we can associate it with an infinitesimal parameter by

\lambda_{\phi}(\omega)=\phi(\omega,\begin{pmatrix}|\omega|^{1/2}&\\
&|\omega|^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}),\quad\omega\in W_{F}

If we fix an infinitesimal parameter
\lambda
, let

P(\lambda,{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}}):=\{\phi\in P({{}^{L}\mathcal{G}})|\lambda_{\phi}=\lambda\}

and

\Xi(\lambda,{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}}):=\{(\phi,\rho)|\phi\in P(\lambda,{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}})/Z_{{G}}(\lambda),\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}(\pi_{0}(Z_{{G}}(\phi)))\}

By varying over all conjugacy classes of infinitesimal parameter of
\mathcal{G}
, we get

\Xi({{}^{L}\mathcal{G}}):=\{(\phi,\rho)|\phi\in\Phi(\mathcal{G}),\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}(\pi_{0}(Z_{{G}}(\phi)))\}

Hence, from the local Langlands correspondence, there is a natural bijection between
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})^{[\mathcal{T},1_{\mathrm{triv}}]}
and the subset of
\Xi(^{L}G)
whose Langlands parameter is trivial on the inertial group
I_{F}
.

We can choose
\phi\in\Phi(\mathcal{G})
such that
t^{\prime}=\phi(\mathrm{Fr})=\times_{n_{1}}(p^{z_{1}})\times\cdots\times\times_{n_{k}}(p^{z_{k}})
satisfies

Condition 5.2
.

|p^{z_{1}}|\geq|p^{z_{2}}|\geq\cdots\geq|p^{z_{k}}|

Let
y=d_{\phi}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
,
t=\lambda_{\phi}(\mathrm{Fr})
,
\bar{t}=-\mathrm{log}t^{\prime}_{h}+d_{\nu}\begin{pmatrix}r_{0}&0\\
0&-r_{0}\end{pmatrix}=-\mathrm{log}|t^{\prime}|+d_{\nu}\begin{pmatrix}r_{0}&0\\
0&-r_{0}\end{pmatrix}
and
r_{0}=\mathrm{log}p^{1/2}
.

Denote

(5.7)

\displaystyle E(\phi,1)

\displaystyle:=(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}})^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1}

\displaystyle M(\phi,1)

\displaystyle:=(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}})^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}M_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1}

Let
\varrho_{0}=|\cdot|_{F}^{z_{i}}
,
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{0}|\cdot|_{F}^{\frac{1-n_{i}}{2}}
,
\cdots
,
\varrho_{n_{i}}=\varrho_{0}|\cdot|_{F}^{\frac{n_{i}-1}{2}}
, and
\Delta_{i}=[\varrho_{1},\varrho_{n_{i}}]
be a segment. Then
(\Delta_{1},\cdots,\Delta_{k})
is an ordered sequence of segments. We have

(5.8)

\displaystyle E(\phi,1)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{k}\rangle

\displaystyle M(\phi,1)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{cos}(\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{k}\rangle)

5.2.
Disconnected case

Recall
\mathcal{G}^{+}=\mathcal{G}\rtimes\Gamma
, where
\Gamma=\langle\gamma\rangle
, and
G^{+}:=G\rtimes\hat{\Gamma}
, where
\hat{\Gamma}=\langle\hat{\gamma}\rangle
.
Now we define

\Xi(\lambda,{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}^{+}}):=\{(\phi,\rho)|\phi\in P(\lambda,{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}})/Z_{{G^{+}}}(\lambda),\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}(\pi_{0}(Z_{{G^{+}}}(\phi)))\}

By varying over all conjugacy classes of infinitesimal parameters of
\mathcal{G}
, we get
\Xi({{}^{L}\mathcal{G}^{+}})
.

Note that
t=\lambda_{\phi}(\mathrm{Fr})
,
t^{\prime}=\phi(\mathrm{Fr})
,
\bar{t}=-\mathrm{log}|t^{\prime}|+d_{\nu}\begin{pmatrix}r_{0}&0\\
0&-r_{0}\end{pmatrix}
,
r_{0}=\mathrm{log}p^{1/2}
, and
Z_{{G^{+}}}(\phi)=Z_{G}(t,y)=Z_{Z_{G}(t_{c})}(\bar{t},y)
. Using what have been proved in section 2 of this paper, we have

(5.9)

\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+}:\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G}^{+})^{[\mathcal{T},1_{\mathrm{triv}}]}\simeq\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G}^{+})^{(\mathcal{J},1_{\mathrm{triv}})}\simeq\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{J}\backslash\mathcal{G}^{+}/\mathcal{J})-\mathrm{Mod}

(5.10)

\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{J}\backslash\mathcal{G}^{+}/\mathcal{J})\simeq\mathcal{H}(G^{+},p^{1/2})

If
\hat{\Gamma}\cdot t\not\subseteq G\cdot t
, then

(5.11)

\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathcal{H}(G,v)}^{\mathcal{H}(G^{+},v)}:\mathcal{H}(G,v)-\mathrm{Mod}_{(t,p^{1/2})}\simeq\mathcal{H}(G^{+},v)-\mathrm{Mod}_{(t,p^{1/2})}

If
\hat{\Gamma}\cdot t\subseteq G\cdot t
, then

(5.12)

\Theta^{+}:\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))-\mathrm{Mod}_{(\mathrm{log}t_{h},\mathrm{log}p^{1/2})}\simeq\mathcal{H}(G^{+},v)-\mathrm{Mod}_{(t,p^{1/2})}

Using the classification of all simple modules of
\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))
obtained in Section 3, we obtain all irreducible representations in
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G}^{+})^{[\mathcal{T},1_{\mathrm{triv}}]}
which are parameterized by
(\phi,\rho)\in\Xi({{}^{L}\mathcal{G}^{+}})
satisfying
\phi(I_{F})=1
.

We have the Iwahori-Matsumoto involution
\mathrm{IM}
on
\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))
given by

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{IM}(N_{w})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sign}(w)N_{w}\quad

\displaystyle(w\in W)

\displaystyle\mathrm{IM}(N_{\tau})

\displaystyle=N_{\tau}\quad

\displaystyle(\tau\in\mathfrak{R})

\displaystyle\mathrm{IM}(\xi)

\displaystyle=-\xi\quad

\displaystyle(\xi\in\mathfrak{t}^{*})

Denote

(5.14)

\displaystyle M^{+}(\phi,1):=\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}M(\phi,1)\quad

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}\cdot t\not\subseteq G\cdot t

(5.15)

\displaystyle M^{+}(\phi,\rho):=(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ(\Theta^{+})\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}M_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},\rho}^{+}\quad

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}\cdot t\subseteq G\cdot t

where
y=d_{\phi}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
,
t=\lambda_{\phi}(\mathrm{Fr})
,
t^{\prime}=\phi(\mathrm{Fr})
,
\bar{t}=-\mathrm{log}|t^{\prime}|+d_{\nu}\begin{pmatrix}r_{0}&0\\
0&-r_{0}\end{pmatrix}
, and
\rho\in\mathrm{Irr}(\pi_{0}(Z_{Z_{G}(t_{c})}(\bar{t},y)))=\mathrm{Irr}(\pi_{0}(Z_{{G^{+}}}(\phi)))
.

Hence we get a Langlands classification of
\mathrm{Irr}(\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G}^{+})^{[T,1_{\mathrm{triv}}]})
using enhanced Langlands parameters that are trivial on
I_{F}
.

If
\mathrm{Res}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}_{\mathcal{G}}M^{+}(\phi,\rho)
is reducible, we have
M^{+}(\phi,\rho)\simeq\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}M(\phi,1)
. We define the twisted standard representation

(5.16)

E^{+}(\phi,1):=\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}E(\phi,1)

Hence if
\hat{\Gamma}\cdot t\not\subseteq G\cdot t
, we have

(5.17)

\displaystyle E^{+}(\phi,1)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}E(\phi,1)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}\circ(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}})^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1}

\displaystyle\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathcal{H}(G,v)}^{\mathcal{H}(G^{+},v)}(\Theta\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1})

If
\hat{\Gamma}\cdot t\subseteq G\cdot t
, we have

(5.18)

\displaystyle E^{+}(\phi,1)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}E(\phi,1)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}\circ(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}})^{-1}\circ\Theta\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1}

\displaystyle\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}\circ\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{H}(Z_{G}(t_{c}))}^{\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))}\mathrm{IM}^{*}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1}

\displaystyle\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{H}(Z_{G}(t_{c}))}^{\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1}

\displaystyle\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1}^{+}

If
\mathrm{Res}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}_{\mathcal{G}}M^{+}(\phi,\rho)
is irreducible, then
\mathrm{Res}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}_{\mathcal{G}}M^{+}(\phi,\rho)=M(\phi,1)
and
M(\phi,1)\simeq\gamma^{*}M(\phi,1)
, where
\gamma^{*}M(\phi,1)
is given by twisting the representation
M(\phi,1)
with the action
\gamma
.

We choose
\phi\in\Phi(\mathcal{G})
such that
t^{\prime}=\phi(\mathrm{Fr})
satisfies Condition
5.2
. Then we have an ordered sequence of segments
(\Delta_{1},\cdots,\Delta_{k})
such that
M(\phi,1)\simeq\mathrm{cos}(\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{1}\rangle\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{k}\rangle)

where
\varrho_{0}=|\cdot|_{F}^{z_{i}}
,
\varrho_{1}=\varrho_{0}|\cdot|_{F}^{\frac{1-n_{i}}{2}}
,
\cdots
,
\varrho_{n_{i}}=\varrho_{0}|\cdot|_{F}^{\frac{n_{i}-1}{2}}
, and a segment
\Delta_{i}=[\varrho_{1},\varrho_{n_{i}}]
. For convenience, we write
\mathrm{St}\langle\Delta_{i}\rangle
as
\mathrm{St}(z_{i},n_{i})
:

(5.19)

\displaystyle M(\phi,1)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{cos}(\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k}))

Recall that
\hat{\Gamma}
acts on
G=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, and from the representation theory of the graded Hecke algebra (Proposition
4.25
) we have
{\gamma^{*}(M(\phi,1))}\simeq M(\hat{\gamma}(\phi),1)
.

Then

(5.20)

\displaystyle M(\hat{\gamma}(\phi),1)\simeq\mathrm{cos}(\mathrm{St}(-z_{k},n_{k})\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}(-z_{1},n_{1}))

Hence, if
M(\phi,1)\simeq\gamma^{*}M(\phi,1)
, then for every
(z_{i},n_{i})
either
p^{z_{i}}={(p^{z_{i}})}^{-1}
or
p^{z_{j}}=(p^{z_{i}})^{-1}
with
n_{j}=n_{i}
for some
(z_{j},n_{j})
.

Remark 5.3
.

From
[
3
, Theorem 7.3]
, we know that if
\pi\in\mathrm{Rep}(\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))
is an irreducible representation, then
{\gamma^{*}(\pi)}
is the contragredient representation of
\pi
.

Note that
t^{\prime}=\phi(\mathrm{Fr})=\times_{n_{1}}(p^{z_{1}})\times\cdots\times\times_{n_{k}}(p^{z_{k}})
satisfies

(5.21)

\displaystyle|p^{z_{1}}|\geq|p^{z_{2}}|\geq\cdots\geq|p^{z_{k}}|

Then either
p^{z_{i}}=(p^{z_{i}})^{-1}
or
p^{z_{k+1-i}}=(p^{z_{i}})^{-1}
with
n_{k+1-i}=n_{i}
.

Pick those
(z_{i},n_{i})
satisfying
p^{z_{i}}=\pm 1
and write

(5.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{0}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{1}}+\cdots+n_{i_{l}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})

Hence we have

(5.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\times\cdots\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{k}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})

where
n_{k+1-i}=n_{i}
.

Then
\gamma
normalizes
\mathcal{Q}
, and we can define
\mathcal{Q}^{+}=\mathcal{Q}\rtimes\Gamma
. Hence we have an irreducible representation
M^{+}(\phi_{\mathcal{Q}},\rho)
of
\mathcal{Q}^{+}

Take the twisted parabolic subgroup
\mathcal{P}^{+}
which contains the upper triangular Borel subgroup and
\mathcal{Q}^{+}
.

We define the twisted standard representation

(5.24)

E^{+}(\phi,\rho):=i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}M^{+}(\phi_{\mathcal{Q}},\rho)

Denote by
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
the Lie algebra of
G
, and define the Vogan moduli space

V(\lambda,{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}}):=\{x\in\hat{\mathfrak{g}}|\mathrm{Ad}(\lambda(\mathrm{Fr}))x=px\}

Let
\mathrm{Per}(\lambda,{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}}^{+})
be the category of
Z_{{G}^{+}}(\lambda)
-equivariant perverse sheaves on
V(\lambda,{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}})
. Then we can parametrize simple objects
\mathrm{IC}(\zeta^{+})
in this category by
\zeta^{+}=(\phi(\zeta^{+}),\rho(\zeta^{+}))\in\Xi(\lambda,{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}^{+}})
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Taking
\xi^{+},\zeta^{+}\in\Xi(\lambda,{{}^{L}\mathcal{G}^{+}})
, we have irreducible representation
M^{+}(\phi(\xi^{+}),\rho(\xi^{+}))
and standard representation
E^{+}(\phi(\zeta^{+}),\rho(\zeta^{+}))
. Then the multiplicity in the Grothendieck group is given by

(5.25)

m(M^{+}(\phi(\xi^{+}),\rho(\xi^{+})),E^{+}(\phi(\zeta^{+}),\rho(\zeta^{+})))=\sum_{k}\mathrm{dim}H^{k}(i_{y(\zeta^{+})}^{!}\mathrm{IC}(\xi^{+})))^{\rho(\zeta^{+})}

where
y=d_{\phi(\zeta^{+})}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
.

Proof.

Denote
t=\lambda_{\phi}(\mathrm{Fr})
.

If
\hat{\Gamma}\cdot t\not\subseteq G\cdot t
, we have
M^{+}(\phi,1):=\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}M(\phi,1)
and
E^{+}(\phi,1):=\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}E(\phi,1)
. Using (
2.16
), (
5.9
) and (
5.12
), we can reduce the disconnected case to the connected case, and get the theorem.

If
\hat{\Gamma}\cdot t\subseteq G\cdot t
, we have

(5.26)

\displaystyle M^{+}(\phi,\rho):=(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}M_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},\rho}^{+}

If
M(\phi,1)\simeq\gamma^{*}M(\phi,1)
, we have defined

(5.27)

\displaystyle E^{+}(\phi,\rho)=i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}M^{+}(\phi_{\mathcal{Q}},\rho)

The irreducible representation
M^{+}(\phi_{\mathcal{Q}},\rho)
of
\mathcal{Q}
is given by

(5.28)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{+}_{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}:\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{Q}^{+})^{(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}},1_{\mathrm{triv}})}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}\backslash\mathcal{Q}^{+}/\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}})-\mathrm{Mod}

(5.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}\backslash\mathcal{Q}^{+}/\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}})\simeq\mathcal{H}(Q^{+},p^{1/2})\simeq\mathcal{H}(Q,p^{1/2})\rtimes\Gamma

and

\Theta^{+}_{Q}:\mathcal{H}(Q^{+},v)-\mathrm{Mod}_{(t,p^{1/2})}\simeq\mathbb{H}(Z_{Q^{+}}(t_{c}))-\mathrm{Mod}_{(\mathrm{log}t_{h},\mathrm{log}p^{1/2})}

which means

(5.30)

\displaystyle M^{+}(\phi_{\mathcal{Q}},\rho)=(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J_{Q}}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}_{Q}\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}M_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},\rho}^{Q,+}

From (
2.32
) and Theorem
3.9
, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G}^{+})^{(\mathcal{J},1_{\mathrm{triv}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(G^{+},p^{1/2})-\mathrm{Mod}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{Q}^{+})^{(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}},1_{\mathrm{triv}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(Q^{+},p^{1/2})-\mathrm{Mod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{+}_{\mathcal{J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{+}_{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ind}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(Q^{+},v)-\mathrm{Mod}_{(t,p^{1/2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}(Z_{Q^{+}}(t_{c}))-\mathrm{Mod}_{(\mathrm{log}t_{h},\mathrm{log}p^{1/2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(Q^{+},v)-\mathrm{Mod}_{(t,p^{1/2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}(Z_{Q^{+}}(t_{c}))-\mathrm{Mod}_{(\mathrm{log}t_{h},\mathrm{log}p^{1/2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{+}_{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ind}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Ind}}

Hence we have

\displaystyle E^{+}(\phi,\rho)

\displaystyle=i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}M^{+}(\phi_{\mathcal{Q}},\rho)

\displaystyle=i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J_{Q}}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}_{Q}\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}M_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},\rho}^{Q,+}

\displaystyle\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}\circ\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{H}(Z_{Q^{+}}(t_{c}))}^{\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))}\mathrm{IM}^{*}M_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},\rho}^{Q,+}

(5.31)

\displaystyle\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{H}(Z_{Q^{+}}(t_{c}))}^{\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))}M_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},\rho}^{Q,+}

(5.32)

\displaystyle\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbb{H}(Z_{Q^{+}}(t_{c}))}^{\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},\rho}^{Q,+}

(5.33)

\displaystyle\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},\rho}^{+}

Here (
5.31
) follows from a canonical isomorphism of
\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))
-modules:

(5.34)

\displaystyle\mathrm{IM}^{*}(\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))\otimes_{\mathbb{H}(Z_{Q^{+}}(t_{c}))}V)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathbb{H}(Z_{G^{+}}(t_{c}))\otimes_{\mathbb{H}(Z_{Q^{+}}(t_{c}))}\mathrm{IM}^{*}(V)

\displaystyle h\otimes v

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{IM}(h)\otimes v

The equality (
5.32
) follows from the fact that
y
lies in the open orbit.
The equality (
5.33
) follows from the induction Theorem
4.23
for graded Hecke algebra.

If
M(\phi,1)\not\simeq\gamma^{*}M(\phi,1)
, we also have

(5.35)

\displaystyle E^{+}(\phi,\rho)\simeq(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}E_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},1}^{+}

which is given by (
5.18
).
Recall that
M^{+}(\phi,\rho)=(\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}^{+})^{-1}\circ\Theta^{+}\circ\mathrm{IM}^{*}M_{y,\bar{t},r_{0},\rho}^{+}
.
Hence using Proposition
4.17
, we get the theorem when
\hat{\Gamma}\cdot t\subseteq G\cdot t
.
∎

6.
Whittaker normalization

In the last section, for any irreducible representation
M(\phi,1)
of
\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
that is not
\gamma
-invariant, we can always get the irreducible representation
M^{+}(\phi,1)=\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}M(\phi,1)
and the standard representation
E^{+}(\phi,1)=\mathrm{ind}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{G}^{+}}E(\phi,1)
by induction. However, if
M(\phi,1)
is
\gamma
-invariant, there are two extensions of
M(\phi,1)
. In the previous section, we used geometry to obtain a uniform classification
M^{+}(\phi,1)
and
M^{+}(\phi,-1)
( where 1 denotes the trivial representation of the component group and -1 denotes the sign representation of the component group).

But if
M(\phi,1)
is
\gamma
-invariant, we can also use the Whittaker normalization introduced by Arthur on the
p
-adic side to get a canonical extension
(M(\phi,1))^{+}_{W}
of
\mathcal{G}^{+}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rtimes\Gamma
.
In this section, we will prove that
(M(\phi,1))^{+}_{W}\simeq M^{+}(\phi,1)
.

6.1.
Definition

Recall that
\Pi
denotes the set of simple roots determined by the upper triangular Borel subgroup. The abelian group
\prod_{\alpha\in\Pi}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
is a quotient of
\mathcal{U}
, where
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
is the root subgroup for
\alpha
. Given characters
\varphi_{\alpha}:\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
, their product defines a character on
\prod_{\alpha\in\Pi}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
and hence a character
\varphi
on
\mathcal{U}
. We say that
\varphi
is principal if all the
\varphi_{\alpha}
are non-trivial. We say that
\varphi
is unramified if all the character
\varphi_{\alpha}
are trivial on
\mathscr{O}
but non-trival on
\mathscr{P}^{-1}
.

Since
\gamma
normalizes
\mathcal{U}
, we make such a choice of
\varphi
that
\varphi
is principal, unramified and
\gamma
invariant.

Suppose first that an irreducible representation
\pi\in\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
is tempered. Then we have a Whittaker functional
\mathcal{W}
on
\pi
. By definition,
\mathcal{W}
is a nonzero linear functional on the underlying space
V
of
\pi
such that

\mathcal{W}(\pi(u)v)=\varphi(u)\mathcal{W}(v)

It is unique up to a scalar multiple.

If the representation
\pi
is equivalent to
\gamma^{*}(\pi)
, we can find a nontrivial intertwining operator
I
from
\pi
to
\gamma^{*}(\pi)
, which is unique up to a nonzero scalar multiple. Hence
\mathcal{W}\circ I
is nonzero linear functional on
V
, and it satisfies

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}\circ I(\pi(u)v)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}(\pi(\gamma(u))I(v))

\displaystyle=\varphi(\gamma(u))\mathcal{W}(I(v))

\displaystyle=\varphi(u)\mathcal{W}\circ I(v)

Therefore
\mathcal{W}\circ I
is also a Whittaker functional on
\pi
. It therefore equals
c\mathcal{W}
, for some
c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
. We set

I_{W}:=c^{-1}I

Then
I_{W}
is the unique intertwining operator from
\pi
to
\gamma^{*}(\pi)
such that

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}\circ I_{W}

Hence we can define a representation
(\pi^{+}_{W},V)
of
\mathcal{G}^{+}
by

(6.2)

\displaystyle\pi^{+}_{W}(g,1)\cdot v

\displaystyle=\pi(g)\cdot v

\displaystyle\pi^{+}_{W}(g,\gamma)\cdot v

\displaystyle=\pi(g)\cdot I_{W}(v)

Suppose
\pi
is general irreducible and
\pi
is equivalent to
\gamma^{*}(\pi)
. From the Langlands classification, we have
\pi\simeq M(\phi,1)
for some Langlands parameter
\phi
, which is the unique quotient of the standard module
E(\phi,1)
. Using the argument in the last section, we have

(6.3)

\displaystyle E(\phi,1)=\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})

where either
p^{z_{i}}=(p^{z_{i}})^{-1}
or
p^{z_{k+1-i}}=(p^{z_{i}})^{-1}
with
n_{k+1-i}=n_{i}
.

Pick those
(z_{i},n_{i})
satisfying
p^{z_{i}}=\pm 1
, then we have a tempered representation

(6.4)

\displaystyle\pi^{0}:=\mathrm{St}(z_{i_{1}},n_{i_{1}})\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}(z_{i_{l}},n_{i_{l}})

of

\mathcal{Q}^{0}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{i_{1}}+\cdots+n_{i_{l}}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Recall

\mathcal{Q}=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\times\cdots\times\mathrm{GL}_{n_{k}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

We have the representation

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})

of
\mathcal{Q}
, which satisfies

(6.6)

\displaystyle\gamma^{*}(\mathrm{St}(z_{i},n_{i}))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{St}(z_{k+1-i},n_{k+1-i})

\displaystyle\gamma^{*}(\pi^{0})

\displaystyle\simeq{\pi}^{0}

Hence

(6.7)

\displaystyle E(\phi,1)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})

\displaystyle=i_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k}))

From the tempered case, we have the Whittaker normalized intertwining operator
I_{W,\pi^{0}}
from
\pi^{0}
to
\gamma^{*}(\pi^{0})
. Since

\gamma^{*}(\mathrm{St}(z_{i},n_{i}))\simeq\mathrm{St}(z_{k+1-i})
,
we have a morphism
I_{i}
from the underlying space of
\mathrm{St}(z_{i},n_{i})
to the space
\mathrm{St}(z_{k+1-i},n_{k+1-i})
such that
I_{i}
is an isomorphism from the representation
\gamma^{*}(\mathrm{St}(z_{i},n_{i}))
to
\mathrm{St}(z_{k+1-i},n_{k+1-i})
.

Let
\phi\in\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\times\cdots\times\pi^{0}\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})
, which is the induction of
\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})
. For every
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, we define

(6.8)

\displaystyle I_{W}(\phi)(g):=(I_{1}^{-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes I_{W,\pi_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes I_{1})\iota(\phi(\gamma(g)))

where
\iota
is the isomorphism from
\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})
to
\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})
that swaps the terms.

Hence
I_{W}
intertwines
E(\phi,1)
and
\gamma^{*}E(\phi,1)
, and using
I_{W}
on
E(\phi,1)=\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\times\cdots\times\pi^{0}\times\cdots\times\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})
, we get the representation
(E(\phi,1))^{+}_{W}
of
\mathcal{G}^{+}
.

Remark 6.1
.

From
[
23
]
, this
I_{W}
induces a trivial action on the Whittaker functional of
E(\phi,1)
. Hence this action is the Whittaker-normalized from the definition in the introduction.

Since
\pi\simeq M(\phi,1)
is the unique irreducible quotient of
E(\phi,1)
, we can get an action
I_{W,\pi}
on
\pi\simeq M(\phi,1)
that intertwines
\pi
and
\gamma^{*}(\pi)
. Hence, using this
I_{W,\pi}
, we get the representation
(M(\phi,1))^{+}_{W}
of
\mathcal{G}^{+}
.

Note that
\gamma
normalizes
\mathcal{Q}
and the representation
\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})
is also
\gamma
invariant. We define

(6.9)

\displaystyle I_{\mathcal{Q}}:=(I_{1}^{-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes I_{W,\pi^{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes I_{1})\circ\iota

Hence
I_{\mathcal{Q}}
intertwines
\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})
and
\gamma^{*}(\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k}))
.

Therefore we can get a representation
(\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k}))^{+}
of
\mathcal{Q}^{+}=\mathcal{Q}\rtimes\Gamma
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Denote
\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}:=\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k})
and
\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}:=(\mathrm{St}(z_{1},n_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{St}(z_{k},n_{k}))^{+}
. Then we have
E(\phi,1)=i_{\mathcal{P}}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}
.

There is an isomorphism of representations of
\mathcal{G}^{+}
:

(6.10)

\displaystyle(E(\phi,1))^{+}_{W}\simeq i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}

which means that the representation
i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}
of
\mathcal{G}^{+}
has the same underlying vector space as
i_{\mathcal{P}}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}
, and the
\gamma
action on
i_{\mathcal{P}}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}
is given by (
6.8
).

Proof.

Taking
\phi\in i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}
, we restrict
\phi
as a function on
\mathcal{G}
, which means we have

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}:i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}

\displaystyle\to i_{\mathcal{P}}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto\bar{\phi}

Since

\displaystyle\phi((g,\gamma))

\displaystyle=\phi((1,\gamma)(\gamma(g),1))

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}((1,\gamma))\phi(\gamma(g),1)

(6.12)

\displaystyle=I_{\mathcal{Q}}\circ\bar{\phi}(\gamma(g))

the map
\mathrm{Res}
is an isomorphism. Since
(1,\gamma)\cdot\phi(g,1)=\phi((g,1)\cdot(1,\gamma))=\phi((g,\gamma))
, the
(1,\gamma)
action on
\bar{\phi}
is given by (
6.12
), which coincides with (
6.8
).
∎

Since
M^{+}(\phi,1)
is the unique irreducible quotient of
E^{+}(\phi,1)
, if we want to prove
(M(\phi,1))^{+}_{W}\simeq M^{+}(\phi,1)
, it suffices to prove
(E(\phi,1))^{+}_{W}\simeq E^{+}(\phi,1)
.

Since
E^{+}(\phi,1)=i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}E^{+}(\phi_{\mathcal{Q}},1)=i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}M^{+}(\phi_{\mathcal{Q}},1)
and
(E(\phi,1))^{+}_{W}\simeq i_{\mathcal{P}^{+}}\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}
, we just need to prove
\pi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{+}\simeq M^{+}(\phi_{\mathcal{Q}},1)
.

6.2.
Tempered case

Let
\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. Denote

(6.13)

\displaystyle\pi_{n}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{St}(z,n)\quad p^{z}=1

\displaystyle\pi^{\prime}_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{St}(z^{\prime},n)\quad p^{z^{\prime}}=-1

Hence
z
lies in
(2\pi i/\mathrm{log}p)\mathbb{Z}
and
z^{\prime}
lies in
\pi i/\mathrm{log}p+(2\pi i/\mathrm{log}p)\mathbb{Z}
.

Take an irreducible representation
\pi
in
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})^{[\mathcal{T},1_{\mathrm{triv}}]}
. If
\pi
is a tempered representation,
\pi
has the form

\pi=\pi_{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\pi_{k_{i}}\times\pi^{\prime}_{l_{1}}\times\cdots\times\pi^{\prime}_{l_{j}}

Hence we can view
\pi
as a unique subrepresentation of the unramified principal series:

\pi\hookrightarrow\sigma:=(\times_{\lambda}\rho|\cdot|_{F}^{\lambda})\times(\times_{\lambda^{\prime}}\rho^{\prime}|\cdot|_{F}^{\lambda^{\prime}})

where
\rho=|\cdot|_{F}^{z}
with
p^{z}=1
,
\rho^{\prime}=|\cdot|_{F}^{z^{\prime}}
with
p^{z^{\prime}}=-1
, and
\lambda
,
\lambda^{\prime}
are the half integers, taken in decreasing order.

Denote
k=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{i}
,
l=l_{1}+\cdots+l_{j}
,
\mathcal{M}=\mathrm{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times\mathrm{GL}_{l}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.
Hence
\sigma=i_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathcal{G}}\mathbb{C}_{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}=i_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathcal{G}}i_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{C}_{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}=i_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathcal{G}}\sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
, where
\sigma_{\mathcal{M}}=i_{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{C}_{(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})}

Denote
\pi_{1}=\pi_{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\pi_{k_{i}}
,
\pi_{-1}=\pi^{\prime}_{l_{1}}\times\cdots\times\pi^{\prime}_{l_{j}}
, then
\pi=i_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{-1})
.

6.2.1.
Intertwining operator and Whittaker functional

We mainly use the ideas in
[
17
]
, but translate their right action into our left action situation.

Denote
V_{\mathcal{J}}=c\operatorname{-ind}_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathcal{G}}(1)=C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{J}\backslash\mathcal{G})
,
\mathcal{H}=C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{J}\backslash\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{J})
.
Hence
V_{\mathcal{J}}
is projective generator, and we have
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{End}_{G}(V_{\mathcal{J}})

(\phi\mapsto t_{\phi})
given by left convolution:

t_{\phi}(f)(g)=\int_{\mathcal{G}}\phi(x)f(x^{-1}g)dx

We also can view
V_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathcal{J}}
as an
\mathcal{H}
-module via

\psi\circ f=\int_{\mathcal{G}}\psi(g)\pi(g)fdg

which means

(6.14)

(\psi\circ f)(x)=\int_{\mathcal{G}}\psi(g)f(xg)dg

where
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
,
f\in V_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathcal{J}}
.

Hence we have a left
\mathcal{H}
-module isomorphism

(6.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}V_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto\psi\circ 1_{\mathcal{J}1\mathcal{J}}

Using (
6.14
), we get

(\psi\circ 1_{\mathcal{J}1\mathcal{J}})(x)=\psi(x^{-1})

Hence for every
\psi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
we can define an action on
V_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathcal{J}}

(6.16)

\psi^{\prime}:\psi\circ 1_{\mathcal{J}1\mathcal{J}}\mapsto(\psi\star\psi^{\prime})\circ 1_{\mathcal{J}1\mathcal{J}}

which is a left
\mathcal{H}
-module isomorphism because of (
6.15
).

We have a commutative diagram

(6.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{G}}(V_{\mathcal{J}})^{\mathrm{opp}}\times\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(V_{\mathcal{J}},W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}}(V_{\mathcal{J}},W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\times\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}}(V_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathcal{J}},W^{\mathcal{J}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}}(V_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathcal{J}},W^{\mathcal{J}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\times W^{\mathcal{J}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{{\mathcal{J}}}}

where the second and the third rows are induced by the action on first row.

We have an anti-isomorphism from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
given by
\psi\mapsto\psi^{\prime}
with
\psi(x)=\psi^{\prime}(x^{-1})
. Hence we obtain an isomorphism
\mathcal{H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{G}}(V_{\mathcal{J}})^{\mathrm{opp}}

(\psi\mapsto t_{\psi})
via

t_{\psi}(f)(g)=\int_{\mathcal{G}}\psi(x^{-1})f(x^{-1}g)dx

Then we can check that the multiplication in the middle of the diagram (
6.17
) is given by (
6.16
).

Denote the group ring
R=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0}]
, which is isomorphic to
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0})
. Thus the element
\varpi^{\mu}

(\mu\in X_{*}(\mathbb{T}))
form a
\mathbb{C}
-basis for the vector space
R
. We view
R
as a
\mathcal{T}
-module via the tautological character
\chi_{\mathrm{univ}}:\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0}\to R
sending
\varpi^{\mu}
to
\varpi^{\mu}
.

Let
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{T}\mathcal{U}
be the upper triangular Borel subgroup. We define
i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})
as the space of locally constant
R
-valued functions
f
on
\mathcal{G}
satisfying

f(tug)=\delta(t)^{1/2}t\cdot f(g)

where
\delta
is the modulus character of
\mathcal{B}
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
,
u\in\mathcal{U}
,
g\in\mathcal{G}
, and the group
\mathcal{G}
acts by right translations.

Define the
\mathcal{G}
-projection
\mathfrak{P}:C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{G})\to i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})
by

\mathfrak{P}(f)(g)=\int_{\mathcal{B}}\chi_{\mathrm{univ}}^{-1}\delta^{1/2}(x)f(xg)dx

where
\chi_{\mathrm{univ}}
and
\delta
are trivial on
\mathcal{U}
, and
\mathcal{B}
has the left Haar measure such that
\mathcal{B}\cap\mathcal{J}
has volume 1.

Recall that
\mathcal{G}=\sqcup\mathcal{B}W\mathcal{J}
, and
T_{(\mu,w)}
is the characteristic function of the double coset
\mathcal{J}(\varpi^{-\mu},w)\mathcal{J}
, where
(\varpi^{-\mu},w)\in\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0}\rtimes W
. Set
f_{(\mu,w)}=\mathfrak{P}(T_{(\mu,w)})
. In particular,
f_{(1,w)}
is identically 0 off
\mathcal{B}w\mathcal{J}
and for
x_{1}\in\mathcal{B}
,
x_{2}\in\mathcal{J}
we have

f_{(1,w)}(x_{1}wx_{2})=\chi_{\mathrm{univ}}\delta^{1/2}(x_{1})

From
[
11
]
,
\mathfrak{P}
induces a
\mathcal{G}
-isomorphism from
V_{\mathcal{J}}
to
i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})
. Consequently
\mathfrak{P}^{\mathcal{J}}
induces an
\mathcal{H}
-isomorphism from
V^{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{J}}
to
i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})^{\mathcal{J}}
.

Using (
6.15
), we have a left
\mathcal{H}
-module isomorphism

(6.18)

\displaystyle\Psi:\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})^{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto\psi\circ f_{1}

where
f_{1}=f_{(1,1)}
.

Recall
Y=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathbb{T})
and
Y\simeq\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0}
given by
y\mapsto y(\varpi^{-1})
. From Section 1 of our paper, we have an embedding
\mathbb{C}[Y]\to\mathcal{H}
. Then
\mathcal{H}
becomes a right
\mathbb{C}[Y]
-module via right multiplication. In section 1,
\mathbb{C}[Y]
has basis
\theta_{y}

(y\in Y)
. We define a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra isomorphism

(6.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}[Y]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}R=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0}]

\displaystyle\theta_{y}

\displaystyle\mapsto y(\varpi^{-1})

Thus
\mathcal{H}
can also be viewed as a right
R=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0}]
module via (
6.19
).

Note that
i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})
naturally carries a right
R=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0}]
-module structure. By
[
11
, Lemma 5.10]
, the map
\Psi
in (
6.18
) is also a right
R
-module homomorphism. Combining these facts, we have

Proposition 6.3
.

The map
\Psi:\mathcal{H}\to i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})^{\mathcal{J}}
defined by
\psi\mapsto\psi\circ f_{1}
induces an isomorphism of
(\mathcal{H},R)
-bimodules.

Given an unramified character
\chi:\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
, we consider the normalized parabolic induction
i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi)
. The following is obvious.

Proposition 6.4
.

The map

(6.20)

\displaystyle\Phi:i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})^{\mathcal{J}}\otimes_{R}\mathbb{C}_{\chi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi)^{\mathcal{J}},\quad\Phi(f\otimes z)(g)=z\chi(f(g))

is an isomorphism of left
\mathcal{H}
-modules.

Combine Proposition
6.3
and Proposition
6.4
, we have a left
\mathcal{H}
-module isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\otimes_{R}\mathbb{C}_{\chi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})^{\mathcal{J}}\otimes_{R}\mathbb{C}_{\chi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi)^{\mathcal{J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

Remark 6.5
.

Using (
2.32
), we can also get the same isomorphism
\mathcal{H}\otimes_{R}\mathbb{C}_{\chi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi)^{\mathcal{J}}
.

Recall the multiplication in
\mathcal{H}
:

\theta_{x}T_{s_{\alpha}}-T_{s_{\alpha}}\theta_{s_{\alpha}(x)}=(p-1)\frac{\theta_{x}-\theta_{s_{\alpha}(x)}}{1-\theta_{-\alpha}}

where
\alpha\in\Pi
is a simple root and
s_{\alpha}
is the corresponding simple reflection.

We define an intertwining element

\displaystyle\iota_{s_{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=T_{s_{\alpha}}(1-\theta_{\alpha})+(p-1)\theta_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=(1-\theta_{-\alpha})T_{s_{\alpha}}+(1-p)

where the last equality follows from the special case:

T_{s_{\alpha}}\theta_{\alpha}=\theta_{-\alpha}T_{s_{\alpha}}+(p-1)(1+\theta_{\alpha})

Using (
6.18
) and the same method as in (
6.16
), for every
\psi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
we can define an action on
i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})^{\mathcal{J}}
by

(6.21)

\psi^{\prime}:\psi\circ f_{1}\mapsto(\psi\star\psi^{\prime})\circ f_{1}

which is a left
\mathcal{H}
-module isomorphism.

Taking
\psi^{\prime}=\iota_{s_{\alpha}}
, we define the intertwining operator
I_{s_{\alpha}}
on
i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})^{\mathcal{J}}
via (
6.21
).

Using the equivalence of categories between
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})^{(\mathcal{J},1_{\mathrm{triv}})}
and
\mathcal{H}-\mathrm{Mod}
, we can transfer
I_{s_{\alpha}}
to an intertwining operator on
i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})
.

Let
\varphi
be a principal character on
\mathcal{U}
. Since
R
is a commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra, the inclusion
\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow R
allows us to view
\varphi
as a character with values in
R^{\times}
.

A Whittaker functional on
i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})
is a right
R
-module map

\mathcal{W}:i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})\to R

such that
\mathcal{W}(\pi(u)f)=\varphi(u)\mathcal{W}(f)
for all
u\in\mathcal{U}
and all
f\in i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})
.

Proposition 6.6

(
[
17
]
)
.

Let
\varphi
be a principal character of
\mathcal{U}
. The
R
-module of all Whittaker functionals is free of rank 1.

Denote by
w_{0}
the longest element of
W
. We realize the Whittaker functional
\mathcal{W}
as the unique functional whose restriction to functions
f\in i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})
supported on
\mathcal{B}w_{0}\mathcal{B}
is given by

(6.22)

\mathcal{W}(f)=\int_{\mathcal{U}}f(w_{0}u)\varphi^{-1}(u)du

where
du
denotes the left Haar measure on
\mathcal{U}
that gives measure 1 to
\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{J}
.

From now on, we assume that
\varphi
is principal and unramified.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be the Whittaker functional defined above (
6.22
). Then

\mathcal{W}(I_{s_{\alpha}}\circ f)=\mathcal{W}(f)^{s_{\alpha}}(p-\theta_{-\alpha})

where
\mathcal{W}(f)^{s_{\alpha}}
denotes the usual action of
s_{\alpha}
on
R=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0}]
, and
\theta_{-\alpha}
is viewed as an element of
R
via (
6.19
).

Proof.

Denote
f_{w_{0}}=f_{(1,w_{0})}
,
f_{s_{\alpha}w_{0}}=f_{(1,{s_{\alpha}w_{0}})}
, with
w_{0}
the longest element in
W
. By
[
9
]
,

\mathcal{W}(f_{s_{\alpha}w_{0}}+f_{w_{0}})=1-p^{-1}\alpha(\varpi)
.
From
[
11
, Lemma 5.10]
,

\displaystyle T_{w_{0}^{-1}}\circ f_{1}

\displaystyle=f_{w_{0}}

\displaystyle T_{w_{0}^{-1}s_{\alpha}^{-1}}\circ f_{1}

\displaystyle=f_{s_{\alpha}w_{0}}

Then

\displaystyle I_{s_{\alpha}}\circ(f_{s_{\alpha}w_{0}}+f_{w_{0}})

\displaystyle=((T_{w_{0}^{-1}s_{\alpha}^{-1}}+T_{w_{0}^{-1}})\star\iota_{s_{\alpha}})\circ f_{1}

\displaystyle=((T_{w_{0}^{-1}s_{\alpha}^{-1}}+T_{w_{0}^{-1}})\cdot(T_{s_{\alpha}}(1-\theta_{\alpha})+(p-1)\theta_{\alpha}))\circ f_{1}

\displaystyle=((T_{w_{0}^{-1}s_{\alpha}^{-1}}+T_{w_{0}^{-1}})\cdot(p-\theta_{\alpha}))\circ f_{1}

\displaystyle=(f_{s_{\alpha}w_{0}}+f_{w_{0}})\cdot(p-\alpha(\varpi^{-1}))

Hence

\mathcal{W}(I_{s_{\alpha}}\circ(f_{s_{\alpha}w_{0}}+f_{w_{0}}))=\mathcal{W}((f_{s_{\alpha}w_{0}}+f_{w_{0}}))^{s_{\alpha}}\cdot(p-\alpha(\varpi))

By Proposition
6.6
, we conclude

\mathcal{W}(I_{s_{\alpha}}\circ f)=\mathcal{W}(f)^{s_{\alpha}}(p-\theta_{-\alpha})

∎

In Section 1, we define an action
\gamma
on
\mathcal{G}
which is given by
\gamma(g)=J(^{t}g^{-1})J^{-1}
. This induces an action
\gamma
on
\mathcal{H}=C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{J}\backslash\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{J})
via
\gamma(f)(g)=f(\gamma(g))
.
We can also define an action on
i_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\chi_{\mathrm{univ}})^{\mathcal{J}}
by

\gamma:\psi\circ f_{1}\mapsto\gamma(\psi)\circ f_{1}

and an action
\gamma
on
R=\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{T}^{0}]
induced by the
\gamma
action on
\mathcal{T}
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\varphi
be a character of
\mathcal{U}
invariant under
\gamma
. Then

\mathcal{W}(\gamma(f))=\mathcal{W}(f)^{\gamma}

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\gamma(\pi(u)f)(x)=(\pi(u)f)(\gamma(x))=f(\gamma(x)u)=(\pi(\gamma(u))\gamma(f))(x)

Hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\gamma(\pi(u)f))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}(\pi(\gamma(u))\gamma(f))=\varphi(\gamma(u))\mathcal{W}(\gamma(f))

\displaystyle=\varphi(u)\mathcal{W}(\gamma(f))

By Proposition
6.6
,
\mathcal{W}(\gamma(f))=c\cdot\mathcal{W}(f)^{\gamma}
for some
c\in R
. Take
f_{1}
, then
\mathcal{W}(\gamma(f_{1}))=\mathcal{W}(f_{1})=\mathcal{W}(f_{1})^{\gamma}=p^{-l(w_{0})}
, where
w_{0}
is the longest element in
W
. Hence
c=1
, and the proposition follows.
∎

Recall that the center of
\mathcal{H}
is
R^{W}
. Let
\mathcal{F}
denote the quotient field of the center. Define

{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{F}\otimes_{R^{W}}\mathcal{H}

For a simple reflection
s_{\alpha}
, define a normalized intertwining element in
{}_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{H}
[
27
]

\displaystyle\iota_{s_{\alpha}}^{0}

\displaystyle=\iota_{s_{\alpha}}(p-\theta_{-\alpha})^{-1}

Let
w=s_{1}\cdots s_{m}
be a reduced expression for
w\in W
, where each
s_{i}
is a simple reflection. Set

\iota_{w}=\iota_{s_{1}}\cdots\iota_{s_{m}}

\iota^{0}_{w}=\iota^{0}_{s_{1}}\cdots\iota^{0}_{s_{m}}

Lemma 6.9
.

\iota_{w}^{0}=\iota_{w}\cdot(\prod_{\beta\in R_{w}}(p-\theta_{-\beta}))^{-1}

where
R_{w}
is the set of positive roots
\beta
such that
w^{-1}\beta
is negative.

Proof.

Using the multiplication in
\mathcal{H}
, we have

(6.23)

\iota_{s_{\alpha}}\cdot\theta_{y}=\theta_{s_{\alpha}(y)}\cdot\iota_{s_{\alpha}}

The case
l(w)=1
is clear. Now, we prove it by induction on
l(w)
. If
l(ws_{\alpha})>l(w)
, we have
R_{ws_{\alpha}}=\{\alpha\}\sqcup\{s_{\alpha}(\beta)|\beta\in R_{w}\}
. Denote
n(\beta)=p-\theta_{-\beta}
. We have
\prod_{\beta\in R_{ws_{\alpha}}}n(\beta)=\prod_{\beta\in R_{w}}n(s_{\alpha}(\beta))\cdot n(\alpha)
. By the induction hypothesis, we have
\iota^{0}_{w}=\iota_{w}\cdot(\prod_{\beta\in R_{w}}n(\beta))^{-1}
. Hence combining (
6.23
), we get

\displaystyle\iota^{0}_{ws\alpha}

\displaystyle=\iota^{0}_{w}\iota^{0}_{s\alpha}=\iota_{w}\cdot(\prod_{\beta\in R_{w}}n(\beta))^{-1}\cdot\iota_{s_{\alpha}}\cdot n(\alpha)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\iota_{w}\iota_{s_{\alpha}}(\prod_{\beta\in R_{w}}n(s_{\alpha}(\beta))\cdot n(\alpha))^{-1}

\displaystyle=\iota_{ws_{\alpha}}(\prod_{\beta\in R_{ws_{\alpha}}}n(\beta))^{-1}

∎

Recall we have

\pi\hookrightarrow\sigma=(\times_{\lambda}\rho|\cdot|_{F}^{\lambda})\times(\times_{\lambda^{\prime}}\rho^{\prime}|\cdot|_{F}^{\lambda^{\prime}})

Denote

Recall that

is the complex dual group of

,

Recall

, where

Then

Recall that we have a tempered representation
\pi
as the unique subrepresentation of
\sigma
, then

restricted to
\pi
is “Whittaker-normalized”.

Recall
\pi=i_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{-1})
. Denote
\bar{V}_{M}=(\pi_{1}\otimes\pi^{\prime}_{-1})^{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{M}}}
. Then
\bar{V}_{M}
is a submodule of
V_{M}
and
\pi^{\mathcal{J}}=\mathcal{H}(G,v)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(M,v)}\bar{V}_{M}
is a submodule of
\sigma^{\mathcal{J}}=V=\mathcal{H}(G,v)\otimes_{\mathcal{H}(M,v)}{V}_{M}
. Recall that we have an action
\hat{\gamma}_{M}
on

which is an isomorphism from

{\hat{\gamma}_{M}^{*}}V_{M}
. Since
\bar{V}_{M}

V_{M}
, the
\hat{\gamma}_{M}

\bar{V}_{M}
and we get the
\hat{\gamma}_{M}

by restriction.

Because the
\hat{\gamma}

is given by
\hat{\gamma}\cdot(g\otimes v)=\hat{\gamma}(g)\iota_{w_{M}^{-1}}^{0}\otimes\hat{\gamma}_{M}(v)
, the restriction of
\hat{\gamma}
on

is also given by

where
